Someone asks me a normal question and I know the answer, but it takes me forever to get it out, or I’m getting up from a chair and it feels like this whole drawn-out thing. And then other times it’s the opposite, where I’m pacing around my apartment and rubbing my hands together, like I’m trying to shake off the feeling after a bad call at work or something. So it’s not constant, but it does keep happening enough that I can’t really ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head or feel compelled to repeat certain actions over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean. Like, I can get stuck thinking about stuff, sure — if something awkward happens at work or I feel like someone noticed me acting weird, I’ll replay it more than I probably should. But I don’t have, like, a specific thought that I have to keep repeating to make it feel right, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd with actions… I mean, I pace sometimes, and I rub my hands together when I’m wound up, like I was saying, but it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because of a rule in my head or because something bad will happen if I don’t. It’s more like I’m uncomfortable and my body just starts doing it. I’m not counting things or checking the door over and over or washing my hands a certain number of times or anything like that. At least, not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not like a phrase repeating over and over, exactly, but I do get stuck on mistakes. Especially work mistakes. Like if I take too long to reply to someone, or I realize later I could’ve worded something better, my brain will keep replaying it after I’m home, even when I’m trying to watch something or go to sleep.\n\nAnd it’s not just, “Oh, that was awkward.” It turns into this whole thing where I’m like, “That proves I’m not good at this,” or, “I let everyone down,” even if it was probably a small thing. With the contract jobs I’ve had too, I’ll think back on one slow response or one little thing I missed and it feels like evidence that I deserved to get fired, or that people were right to be disappointed in me. I know that might sound dramatic when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really true, and it’s hard to make it stop looping.\n\nDoctor: Are these episodes and thoughts interfering with your ability to work or interact with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I’m not completely unable to function — I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just lying in bed every day with everything falling apart. I can still keep a roof over my head, I can still get through some basic things. But work has definitely taken a hit.\n\nWith jobs, especially customer service or contract-type stuff, I’ll miss deadlines or respond too slowly, and then the more anxious or stuck I get about it, the more I avoid calls or messages, which obviously makes it worse. And I know how that looks from the outside — like I don’t care, or I’m flaky, or unreliable. But inside it’s more like I’m frozen or I’m replaying everything I did wrong and then I can’t make myself pick up the phone. So I’ve lost momentum with steady work. I can get something going for a while, but keeping it steady is the hard part.\n\nSocially, it’s kind of similar. I still have people I care about, but I see friends less than I used to. I’ll put off replying, or I’ll feel like I don’t have the energy to explain why I’ve been weird or distant, and then more time passes and it gets harder to reach out. Family too, sometimes — not that I don’t want to talk to them, it just starts feeling like another thing I’m behind on.\n\nAnd basic routines slide more than I like to admit. Meals get irregular, errands pile up, and even stuff I used to like, like going hiking, I don’t really do unless I force myself into it. So… yeah, I’m functioning, but it feels like I’m dragging everything behind me instead of actually living normally.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about everyday things, like work, finances, or relationships, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that, because I do worry about those things, but I don’t know if I’d say it’s for “no reason.” Like with work, money, people… there usually is some kind of reason I can point to, even if maybe I make it bigger in my head than it needs to be.\n\nI guess I don’t sit around worrying about, like, random everyday things out of nowhere all day. It’s more that if something feels uncertain or if I already think I messed something up, then I can get caught in it. But if you mean do I constantly worry about every normal life thing even when things are basically fine… I don’t think that’s exactly it. At least I haven’t noticed it that way.\n\nDoctor: How much distress do these episodes of slowed or sped-up movement, and the thoughts that come with them, cause you on a daily basis?\n\nPatient: It’s… distressing enough that it’s actually one of the reasons I came in. Like, I can handle feeling bad quietly, if that makes sense, but this is different because people can see it.\n\nSome days I feel like I’m moving through water. I’ll be sitting there and someone asks me something, and I know I need to answer, but there’s this delay, like my body and my brain are buffering. Even getting out of a chair can take forever, and I’m aware of it while it’s happening, which makes it worse because then I’m thinking, “They can tell something’s wrong with me.” It’s embarrassing.\n\nAnd then other times it flips the other way. I’ll pace around my apartment after a bad call or something stressful, rubbing my hands, walking back and forth like I’m trying to shake it off physically. It’s not even productive pacing. I’m not solving anything. I’m just wound up and stuck in my own head.\n\nSo on a daily basis… I’d say it’s not constant every second, but when it happens, it really bothers me. It makes me feel exposed, I guess. Like the thoughts are already hard enough, but when my body starts showing it too, I feel like I can’t hide it or pass as normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thoughts or behaviors are attempts to reduce anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d put it that way exactly. Like, I’m trying to think of a clear example, and I’m not sure I have one where I do something over and over because I believe it’ll stop something bad from happening.\n\nThe repetitive thoughts feel more like I get stuck on something — replaying it, going back over what I said or what I should’ve done — but I don’t know that it feels like, “If I think about this enough, then I’ll prevent something.” It’s more like my brain won’t let it go, even when I know going over it again isn’t helping.\n\nAnd with the behaviors, like if I’m pacing or fidgeting or rubbing my hands, it does feel connected to being tense or worked up, but not in a planned way. I’m not thinking, “I need to do this so I’ll be safe,” or anything like that. It’s more automatic, like I realize I’ve been doing it after a while.\n\nSo… maybe it reduces anxiety a tiny bit in the moment, or maybe I’m just trying to get the feeling out of my body, I don’t know. But I haven’t really noticed it as a ritual or a prevention thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus on tasks or activities because your mind keeps wandering or you get easily distracted?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate that from just being tired or not interested in something, honestly. I mean, with some tasks, yeah, my mind can drift, but I don’t know if it’s more than what most people deal with.\n\nLike at work, if I’m doing something repetitive or there’s a lot of noise around me, I might have to reread something or kind of pull myself back to what I was doing. But I wouldn’t say I’m constantly unable to focus, or that I’m distracted by every little thing all the time. It’s more noticeable when I’m already feeling off or when the task is boring.\n\nSo… I guess sometimes, but I haven’t really thought of it as a main problem on its own. It doesn’t feel like the biggest thing compared to the other stuff I’ve been trying to explain.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive thoughts or behaviors are something you can control, or do they feel like they're happening to you without your choice?\n\nPatient: It feels like… kind of both, but mostly like it’s happening to me before I’ve really chosen it.\n\nLike when I’m moving slow, that doesn’t feel voluntary at all. I’m not sitting there deciding to take forever to answer someone or deciding to make getting out of a chair into this whole embarrassing production. It’s more like I know what I need to do, and then my body just isn’t catching up. I’m aware of it, which makes me feel even more self-conscious, but awareness doesn’t mean I can just snap out of it.\n\nAnd with the pacing or rubbing my hands, I guess technically I can stop if I notice it. Like, if I suddenly realize I’ve been pacing around my apartment after a bad call, I can sit down for a minute. But it doesn’t feel like I started it on purpose. It’s like my body starts trying to shake something off before I’ve made a decision. Then if I stop, the urge or the tension is still there, so sometimes I just end up doing it again without meaning to.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in, honestly. If it was just thoughts inside my head, I could maybe hide it better, but when I’m either moving like I’m underwater or walking back and forth rubbing my hands, people can see something is off. It doesn’t feel fully under my control.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take time off work or avoid social situations because of these episodes of slowed or sped-up movement and the thoughts that come with them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not in the sense that I’ve completely stopped functioning or anything. I’m still paying rent, I’m still keeping a roof over my head, and I can get through things when I really have to. But it has definitely affected work and seeing people.\n\nWith work, it’s more like I’ve lost momentum. I miss deadlines sometimes, or I put off calls because I just can’t make myself get into that mode, especially if I’m feeling slowed down or if my thoughts are kind of stuck looping. And then when I do show up, I worry I come across as unreliable, because I’m not always as quick or responsive as I’m supposed to be. Customer service is kind of hard to fake your way through when your timing is off, you know? People can hear it if you’re slow to respond or if you sound distracted.\n\nSocially, I’ve been seeing friends less. I don’t always cancel dramatically or anything, but I’ll just avoid making plans, or I’ll let messages sit too long, and then it feels awkward to respond. Basic routines slide too — meals, errands, stuff like that. Even hiking, which I used to do more, I kind of have to force myself now instead of just naturally wanting to go.\n\nSo, yes, I guess I have avoided things and it has gotten in the way. It’s not like my life has fallen apart, but it’s definitely made me less steady.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily life or relationships due to these episodes and thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely unable to live my life, because I’m not. I’m still managing the basics in the broad sense — I still have a place to live, I’m still keeping a roof over my head, I’m not just totally disappearing from everything. But it’s like I’ve lost this steady momentum I used to have.\n\nWork is probably where I notice it most, because if I’m slowed down or caught up in my head, I’ll miss deadlines or put off making calls. And then once I’ve avoided one call, it kind of turns into avoiding the next one too, because I’m already anxious or embarrassed that I’m behind. I worry I come across as unreliable, even if I’m trying. In customer service, you kind of have to be responsive and sound present, and when I’m not, I can tell it affects how I come across.\n\nAnd relationships… yeah, I see friends less. Not always because I actively decide, “I don’t want to see them,” but more because I don’t answer messages right away, or I avoid making plans, and then too much time passes and it feels awkward. So I end up more isolated than I mean to be.\n\nEven normal routines slide. Meals get irregular, errands pile up, and hiking — which used to be something I’d just do because it helped — now feels like something I have to force myself into. So it’s not like everything has collapsed, but it’s definitely affecting my day-to-day life and how connected I feel to people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these episodes and thoughts are significantly interfering with your ability to maintain relationships or perform at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think… yes. Significantly, but not in a total disaster kind of way, if that makes sense. I can still keep a roof over my head. I’m not unable to function completely. But I’ve definitely lost momentum, especially with work.\n\nLike, I’ll miss deadlines or I’ll put off calls because I just can’t get myself into the right state to do them. And then once I avoid one call, it turns into this whole thing where I feel behind and embarrassed, so I avoid more. I know from the outside that probably looks like I’m being unreliable or careless, but it doesn’t feel that simple from the inside. I’m trying, but I’m not as steady as I used to be.\n\nWith friends, it’s similar. Then I feel awkward and avoid it more. So I end up seeing people less than I want to.\n\nEven basic stuff slides, like meals or errands, and hiking too. That used to be something I’d do more naturally, and now I have to really force myself to go. So… yeah, it’s interfering. I’m still managing, but I don’t feel like I’m maintaining things very well.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your repetitive thoughts or behaviors are primarily focused on specific worries or concerns, such as making mistakes or not meeting expectations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s actually a big part of it. It’s not usually like some random ritual or anything. It’s more that I get stuck on these specific worries that I messed something up or didn’t meet expectations.\n\nLike with work, I’ll replay a small thing over and over — a reply I sent too slowly, or a call I didn’t handle as smoothly as I should have, or a message from one of my contract jobs where I think maybe I sounded unprofessional. And it can be such a minor thing, like objectively I know it might not even matter that much, but in my head it turns into, “That proves I’m bad at this,” or “I let everyone down,” or “They were right to be disappointed in me.”\n\nSometimes it goes even further, where I’ll think, “Honestly, maybe I deserved to be fired,” even if the actual mistake was just being slow to respond or missing some expectation that maybe no one else is even thinking about anymore. I’ll keep going back through it, almost like if I analyze it enough I can figure out exactly where I failed. But it doesn’t really resolve. It just makes me feel worse and more guilty.\n\nSo yeah, it’s definitely focused on mistakes and not measuring up. That’s probably the main theme.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thoughts are intrusive, meaning they enter your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do feel intrusive. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, I’m going to think about every little thing I did wrong today.” It just kind of breaks in.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to do something normal, or move on with my day, and suddenly I’m replaying some small mistake from work or one of my contract jobs. A slow reply, or something I worded badly, or a call I didn’t handle the way I think I should have. And then it snowballs really fast into this feeling like that one thing means I let everyone down.\n\nEven if part of me knows it was probably minor, the thought comes in really forcefully, like, “No, this proves you messed up,” or “You deserved to get in trouble for that,” or even, “You probably deserved to be fired.” I don’t want to think that way. I don’t enjoy it. It just shows up, and then I get stuck trying to argue with it or prove to myself it wasn’t that bad, but that doesn’t always work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thoughts are attempts to reduce anxiety or prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about perceived mistakes or flaws?\n\nPatient: I think they’re more about perceived mistakes or flaws, honestly. It doesn’t feel like I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening in the future, exactly. It’s more like I’m trying to figure out why I’m… deficient, I guess. Like what I did wrong, where I failed, why I wasn’t good enough.\n\nA lot of it is work-related. I’ll replay these small things from my regular job or contract work — like if I replied slowly to someone, or I didn’t handle something as well as I think I should have, or I missed some expectation. And then my brain treats it like this huge evidence against me, like, “See, you let everyone down,” or, “This is why people lose patience with you.”\n\nSometimes it gets really harsh, where I’ll think, “Maybe I deserved to be fired,” even if the actual thing was just one slow reply or one awkward interaction. I know that probably sounds exaggerated, and part of me can see that, but emotionally it feels true in the moment. So I keep going back over it, not really because I think it will stop something from happening, but because I feel guilty and like I need to prove I’m not as bad as I feel… except I usually end up feeling worse.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. And it can be such a minor thing, like objectively I know it might not even matter that much, but in my head it turns into, “That proves I’m bad at this,” or “I let everyone down,” or “They were right to be disappointed in me.”\n\nSometimes it goes even further, where I’ll think, “Honestly, maybe I deserved to be fired,” even if the actual mistake was just being slow to respond or missing some expectation that maybe no one else is even thinking about anymore. I’ll keep going back through it, almost like if I analyze it enough I can figure out exactly where I failed. But it doesn’t really resolve. It just makes me feel worse and more guilty.\n\nSo yeah, it’s definitely focused on mistakes and not measuring up. That’s probably the main theme.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thoughts are intrusive, meaning they enter your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do feel intrusive. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, I’m going to think about every little thing I did wrong today.” It just kind of breaks in.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to do something normal, or move on with my day, and suddenly I’m replaying some small mistake from work or one of my contract jobs. A slow reply, or something I worded badly, or a call I didn’t handle the way I think I should have. And then it snowballs really fast into this feeling like that one thing means I let everyone down.\n\nEven if part of me knows it was probably minor, the thought comes in really forcefully, like, “No, this proves you messed up,” or “You deserved to get in trouble for that,” or even, “You probably deserved to be fired.” I don’t want to think that way. I don’t enjoy it. It just shows up, and then I get stuck trying to argue with it or prove to myself it wasn’t that bad, but that doesn’t always work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thoughts are attempts to reduce anxiety or prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about perceived mistakes or flaws?\n\nPatient: I think they’re more about perceived mistakes or flaws, honestly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… it is. I mean, I’m still functioning in the basic sense. I have a place to live, I can keep things from totally falling apart, so it’s not like my whole life has collapsed. But it definitely affects the quality of it.

Work is probably the biggest place I notice it. I’ve lost a lot of momentum with steady work because I’ll miss deadlines, or I’ll avoid calls that I know I need to make, and then that makes me look unreliable. And the frustrating thing is, I’m not trying to be careless. I’ll know something needs to be done, but I get stuck, or I put it off, or I overthink it, and then suddenly I’ve made the situation worse. So even when I’m technically still getting by, I don’t feel like I’m building anything stable.

And outside of work, I’ve pulled back more than I like to admit. I don’t see friends as much. I’ll tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but then weeks go by and I realize I haven’t really made the effort. Basic routines slip too — meals, errands, getting outside, even hiking, which used to be something that helped me feel more normal. Now I have to really force myself to do those things instead of them just being part of life.

So yeah, I’d say it’s affecting my life. Maybe mildly compared to how bad things could be, but it’s enough that I don’t feel like I’m living the way I should be.

I don’t enjoy it. It just shows up, and then I get stuck trying to argue with it or prove to myself it wasn’t that bad, but that doesn’t always work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thoughts are attempts to reduce anxiety or prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about perceived mistakes or flaws?\n\nPatient: I think they’re more about perceived mistakes or flaws, honestly. It doesn’t feel like I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening in the future, exactly. It’s more like I’m trying to figure out why I’m… deficient, I guess. Like what I did wrong, where I failed, why I wasn’t good enough.\n\nA lot of it is work-related. I’ll replay these small things from my regular job or contract work — like if I replied slowly to someone, or I didn’t handle something as well as I think I should have, or I missed some expectation. And then my brain treats it like this huge evidence against me, like, “See, you let everyone down,” or, “This is why people lose patience with you.”\n\nSometimes it gets really harsh, where I’ll think, “Maybe I deserved to be fired,” even if the actual thing was just one slow reply or one awkward interaction. I know that probably sounds exaggerated, and part of me can see that, but emotionally it feels true in the moment. So I keep going back over it, not really because I think it will stop something from happening, but because I feel guilty and like I need to prove I’m not as bad as I feel… except I usually end up feeling worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I’m still functioning in the basic sense. I have a place to live, I can keep things from totally falling apart, so it’s not like my whole life has collapsed. I’ll know something needs to be done, but I get stuck, or I put it off, or I overthink it, and then suddenly I’ve made the situation worse. So even when I’m technically still getting by, I don’t feel like I’m building anything stable.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back more than I like to admit. I don’t see friends as much. I’ll tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but then weeks go by and I realize I haven’t really made the effort. Basic routines slip too — meals, errands, getting outside, even hiking, which used to be something that helped me feel more normal. Now I have to really force myself to do those things instead of them just being part of life.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say it’s affecting my life. Maybe mildly compared to how bad things could be, but it’s enough that I don’t feel like I’m living the way I should be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ve lost a lot of momentum with steady work because I’ll miss deadlines, or I’ll avoid calls that I know I need to make, and then that makes me look unreliable. And the frustrating thing is, I’m not trying to be careless. I’ll know something needs to be done, but I get stuck, or I put it off, or I overthink it, and then suddenly I’ve made the situation worse. So even when I’m technically still getting by, I don’t feel like I’m building anything stable.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back more than I like to admit. I don’t see friends as much. Basic routines slip too — meals, errands, getting outside, even hiking, which used to be something that helped me feel more normal. Now I have to really force myself to do those things instead of them just being part of life.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say it’s affecting my life. Maybe mildly compared to how bad things could be, but it’s enough that I don’t feel like I’m living the way I should be.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever felt a strong urge to perform a specific ritual or behavior to alleviate anxiety or prevent a perceived disaster?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don’t think so… at least not in the way you’re describing it, like a ritual I feel I have to do so something terrible won’t happen.

I’ll replay these small things from my regular job or contract work — like if I replied slowly to someone, or I didn’t handle something as well as I think I should have, or I missed some expectation. I know that probably sounds exaggerated, and part of me can see that, but emotionally it feels true in the moment. So I keep going back over it, not really because I think it will stop something from happening, but because I feel guilty and like I need to prove I’m not as bad as I feel… except I usually end up feeling worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I’m still functioning in the basic sense. I have a place to live, I can keep things from totally falling apart, so it’s not like my whole life has collapsed. But it definitely affects the quality of it.\n\nWork is probably the biggest place I notice it. I’ve lost a lot of momentum with steady work because I’ll miss deadlines, or I’ll avoid calls that I know I need to make, and then that makes me look unreliable. And the frustrating thing is, I’m not trying to be careless. I’ll know something needs to be done, but I get stuck, or I put it off, or I overthink it, and then suddenly I’ve made the situation worse. So even when I’m technically still getting by, I don’t feel like I’m building anything stable.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back more than I like to admit. I don’t see friends as much. I’ll tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but then weeks go by and I realize I haven’t really made the effort. Basic routines slip too — meals, errands, getting outside, even hiking, which used to be something that helped me feel more normal. Now I have to really force myself to do those things instead of them just being part of life.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say it’s affecting my life. Maybe mildly compared to how bad things could be, but it’s enough that I don’t feel like I’m living the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a strong urge to perform a specific ritual or behavior to alleviate anxiety or prevent a perceived disaster?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way you’re describing it, like a ritual I feel I have to do so something terrible won’t happen.\n\nI mean, I’ll definitely get stuck going back over things in my head, and sometimes I’ll check a message or reread something because I’m worried I said it wrong, but it doesn’t feel like, “If I don’t do this exact behavior, there’s going to be a disaster.” It’s more like I’m uncomfortable and trying to reassure myself, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t have a specific routine or action, like counting or touching things or doing something a certain number of times. Nothing like that really stands out to me. It’s more mental than behavioral, and even then it feels more like guilt or second-guessing than preventing something from happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It doesn’t feel like I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening in the future, exactly. It’s more like I’m trying to figure out why I’m… deficient, I guess. Like what I did wrong, where I failed, why I wasn’t good enough.\n\nA lot of it is work-related. I’ll replay these small things from my regular job or contract work — like if I replied slowly to someone, or I didn’t handle something as well as I think I should have, or I missed some expectation. I don’t think I do, no. Not in a rigid way, at least. Like, I don’t have a rule where I have to repeat something a certain number of times, or say a phrase in my head, or redo an action until it feels “right.” I’m trying to think if there’s anything like that, because sometimes I do get mentally stuck on things, but it doesn’t really feel like a compulsion.

It’s not like my life has fallen apart, but it’s definitely made me less steady.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant impact on your daily life or relationships due to these episodes and thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely unable to live my life, because I’m not. Not always because I actively decide, “I don’t want to see them,” but more because I don’t answer messages right away, or I avoid making plans, and then too much time passes and it feels awkward. So I end up more isolated than I mean to be.\n\nEven normal routines slide. Meals get irregular, errands pile up, and hiking — which used to be something I’d just do because it helped — now feels like something I have to force myself into. So it’s not like everything has collapsed, but it’s definitely affecting my day-to-day life and how connected I feel to people.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these episodes and thoughts are significantly interfering with your ability to maintain relationships or perform at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think… yes. Significantly, but not in a total disaster kind of way, if that makes sense. I can still keep a roof over my head. I’m not unable to function completely. But I’ve definitely lost momentum, especially with work.\n\nLike, I’ll miss deadlines or I’ll put off calls because I just can’t get myself into the right state to do them. And then once I avoid one call, it turns into this whole thing where I feel behind and embarrassed, so I avoid more. I know from the outside that probably looks like I’m being unreliable or careless, but it doesn’t feel that simple from the inside. I’m trying, but I’m not as steady as I used to be.\n\nWith friends, it’s similar. I don’t usually have some big reason for not seeing them — I just don’t answer messages, or I let plans drift, or I tell myself I’ll reply later and then later becomes a week. Then I feel awkward and avoid it more. So I end up seeing people less than I want to.\n\nEven basic stuff slides, like meals or errands, and hiking too. That used to be something I’d do more naturally, and now I have to really force myself to go. So… yeah, it’s interfering. I’ll keep going back through it, almost like if I analyze it enough I can figure out exactly where I failed. But it doesn’t really resolve. It just makes me feel worse and more guilty.\n\nSo yeah, it’s definitely focused on mistakes and not measuring up. That’s probably the main theme.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thoughts are intrusive, meaning they enter your mind without you wanting them to?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they do feel intrusive. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “Okay, I’m going to think about every little thing I did wrong today.” It just kind of breaks in.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to do something normal, or move on with my day, and suddenly I’m replaying some small mistake from work or one of my contract jobs. Like what I did wrong, where I failed, why I wasn’t good enough.\n\nA lot of it is work-related. I don’t enjoy it. It just shows up, and then I get stuck trying to argue with it or prove to myself it wasn’t that bad, but that doesn’t always work.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your repetitive thoughts are attempts to reduce anxiety or prevent something bad from happening, or are they more about perceived mistakes or flaws?\n\nPatient: I think they’re more about perceived mistakes or flaws, honestly. It doesn’t feel like I’m trying to prevent something bad from happening in the future, exactly. It’s more like I’m trying to figure out why I’m… deficient, I guess. Like what I did wrong, where I failed, why I wasn’t good enough.\n\nA lot of it is work-related. I’ll replay these small things from my regular job or contract work — like if I replied slowly to someone, or I didn’t handle something as well as I think I should have, or I missed some expectation. And then my brain treats it like this huge evidence against me, like, “See, you let everyone down,” or, “This is why people lose patience with you.”\n\nSometimes it gets really harsh, where I’ll think, “Maybe I deserved to be fired,” even if the actual thing was just one slow reply or one awkward interaction. I know that probably sounds exaggerated, and part of me can see that, but emotionally it feels true in the moment. So I keep going back over it, not really because I think it will stop something from happening, but because I feel guilty and like I need to prove I’m not as bad as I feel… except I usually end up feeling worse.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are affecting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is. I mean, I’m still functioning in the basic sense. I have a place to live, I can keep things from totally falling apart, so it’s not like my whole life has collapsed. But it definitely affects the quality of it.\n\nWork is probably the biggest place I notice it. I’ve lost a lot of momentum with steady work because I’ll miss deadlines, or I’ll avoid calls that I know I need to make, and then that makes me look unreliable. And the frustrating thing is, I’m not trying to be careless. I’ll know something needs to be done, but I get stuck, or I put it off, or I overthink it, and then suddenly I’ve made the situation worse. So even when I’m technically still getting by, I don’t feel like I’m building anything stable.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve pulled back more than I like to admit. I don’t see friends as much. I’ll tell myself I’m just tired or busy, but then weeks go by and I realize I haven’t really made the effort. Basic routines slip too — meals, errands, getting outside, even hiking, which used to be something that helped me feel more normal. Now I have to really force myself to do those things instead of them just being part of life.\n\nSo yeah, I’d say it’s affecting my life. Maybe mildly compared to how bad things could be, but it’s enough that I don’t feel like I’m living the way I should be.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a strong urge to perform a specific ritual or behavior to alleviate anxiety or prevent a perceived disaster?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… at least not in the way you’re describing it, like a ritual I feel I have to do so something terrible won’t happen.\n\nI mean, I’ll definitely get stuck going back over things in my head, and sometimes I’ll check a message or reread something because I’m worried I said it wrong, but it doesn’t feel like, “If I don’t do this exact behavior, there’s going to be a disaster.” It’s more like I’m uncomfortable and trying to reassure myself, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t have a specific routine or action, like counting or touching things or doing something a certain number of times. Nothing like that really stands out to me. It’s more mental than behavioral, and even then it feels more like guilt or second-guessing than preventing something from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel compelled to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to an obsession or according to rules that must be applied rigidly?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I do, no. Not in a rigid way, at least. Like, I don’t have a rule where I have to repeat something a certain number of times, or say a phrase in my head, or redo an action until it feels “right.” I’m trying to think if there’s anything like that, because sometimes I do get mentally stuck on things, but it doesn’t really feel like a compulsion.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll keep thinking about something because it’s bothering me, not because there’s a rule I have to follow. I don’t feel like, “I must do this mental act exactly this way or something bad will happen.” I might reread something or replay it in my head, but it’s not organized like a ritual. It’s just… I guess rumination, or second-guessing. So if you mean repeated actions or mental routines that I feel forced to do in a specific way, I haven’t really noticed that."
  }
